The person, the collection, and the biological records

The first exchange asks what a micro-directory is. The May 2026 definition describes a division of Karbytes For Life Blog with an index page and one corresponding public repository. A macro-directory supplies one route to each such unit. The individual index identifies the repository and lists the files, commonly with the direct file reference visible as the link text. A repository can group material from a period in karbytes’ life or from a shared subject. The source of the index itself may be stored with the files it lists or in a later repository. Reconstructing the whole collection also involves the separate precision-batch indexes and the less conspicuous items called Easter eggs, some of which are outside the main directory listings. The definition presents the precision batches as a way to make file-by-file preservation easier.

That page supplies the current capitalization convention. Lowercase karbytes names Kar Beringer, the human-to-cybernetic information-processing agent who made the collection. Uppercase KARBYTES names the collection of public-domain digital files. The older ABOUT_KARBYTES page uses the lowercase word for both senses. Here the distinction is put to work immediately. The person declaring authorship is karbytes. The transcript, photograph, video, or copy someone encounters belongs to KARBYTES.

The next reviewed page, karbytes_unique_identity, places a biological reference alongside a historical one. The proposed identifying conditions are the particular genome associated with karbytes and original authorship of Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog. The page reproduces a July 2025 screenshot of a public-domain statement, including the claim that files become available for copying on publication and are routinely saved to the Wayback Machine. A genome could be shared, and someone could reproduce the files of either website. The historical condition concerns who originally made the sites at the designated publication locations. The page also qualifies its own uniqueness claim by imagining parallel universes with indistinguishable contents. Within that hypothesis, a matching genome and authorship history could occur more than once.

The transcript examines three representations of the genetic material. The first is the 2023 23andMe genotyping export. According to the archived review, it is 16,330,970 bytes, with twenty comment lines and 643,535 marker records. Each record gives an identifier, chromosome, reference position, and genotype field. The header specifies human reference build 37 and plus-strand reporting. The transcript distinguishes the marker count from the 640,842 distinct chromosome-and-position pairs, since different marker identifiers sometimes share a coordinate. It also reports 631,068 called genotypes and 12,467 undetermined entries. The rows labeled Y all have undetermined calls in that export. The archived answer explains this in terms of what the file records, without treating the presence of a chromosome label as evidence of a successful call. The file is a dense list of selected measurements. Its title uses genome language, while its rows leave the sequence between measured positions unspecified.

The second representation is the ancestry report published with two screenshots. One arranges estimated generational ranges on a timeline. The other shows population percentages and confidence labels for more specific geographic matches. The archived review records European at 60.3 percent and East Asian at 37.7 percent, with smaller displayed categories making up the remainder. Within the larger groups it notes 30.2 percent Scandinavian, 18.0 percent French and German, 25.0 percent Filipino and Austronesian, and 12.4 percent Chinese. It distinguishes a percentage assigned to a broad population from a location match. Norway, for example, appears as a highly likely match within the Scandinavian category, while the percentage is attached to the broader category. The timeline gives yet another kind of estimate, expressed as a range of generations. Keeping the screenshots and raw export together preserves both the data the author chose to publish and how a commercial interface presented its interpretation at the time.

The third representation comes from a 2025 paired-end whole-genome sequencing run. The page called karbytes_full_genome is an index to four micro-directories. The journals summarized in the transcript explain that the publication preserves the first set of reads from the paired-end run, converted from FASTQ to FASTA and compressed. The FASTA retains the read identifiers and nucleotide sequences. The per-base quality scores belong to the original FASTQ, and a chromosome-by-chromosome assembly would be a separate object. The journals report an original first-read file of about 30.8 gigabytes and a compressed FASTA of about 15.7 gigabytes. They also describe a smaller compression experiment and the practical need to accommodate optical-disc capacity and individually archivable files.

The published compressed FASTA was divided into 2,998 consecutive pieces, distributed as 991, 909, 700, and 398 across the four directories. The suffixes run from 0000 through 2997. Padding makes the filename order agree with the numerical order. The boundaries are byte boundaries in the compressed stream. The archived review reports checking that every suffix occurs once and cites karbytes’ separate test in which the original compressed file and the file reconstructed from the pieces produced the same SHA-256 digest. Here the genome enters the document as a preservation problem as well as an identity reference. An extensive record has been given a naming scheme, an index, a way to verify reconstruction, and a documented reason for being divided as it was.

Preserving a collection that can still change

ABOUT_KARBYTES gives the collection a personal audience. Any willing reader can encounter it, while its most specifically intended readers are karbytes’ hypothetical future selves. The page describes selecting experiences and knowledge, encoding the resulting artifacts in files, and copying them as a way to resist forgetting or distortion. It then imagines repeating that process between universes. In the proposed scenario, a wormhole could transmit the exact binary content of a file to a younger or lower-entropy universe. Karbytes also wonders whether a destination universe could begin as a simulation and whether a replica of its body and environment might continue the attention that originally produced the archive. These are the page’s speculative extensions of an ordinary practice. Each time a file is copied successfully, the encoded artifact is available on a different physical substrate. The proposed continuity of a conscious observer raises an additional question.

KARBYTES_CODE_POSSIBLY_STOPS_GROWING and KARBYTES_CODE_CONTINUES ask what can happen within the much smaller scale of a website and its repositories. The first dates the development of its archiving protocol to July 2025. It defines the collection around pages, embedded material, and qualifying linked files associated with the two original sites. It says readers may regard the entire collection as permanently closed after ten years or longer with no change to any constituent file. Karbytes would prefer to remain able to develop the work, preserve it, and direct its growth. The rule provides a way to interpret prolonged inactivity even if its cause is unknown.

KARBYTES_CODE_CONTINUES favors making as few changes to existing files as possible. It reserves edits to the author’s collection to karbytes while allowing copies to circulate. Its version of the inactivity rule says more than ten consecutive years. It also gives a separate potential finalization date for Karlina Object, March 2, 2026. That date pertains to one site, whereas the decade-long condition concerns the whole collection. The page describes locating historical captures by the individual raw-file references and lists ninety-six repositories in the archived count. Eighty are extension packs numbered zero through seventy-nine. The other entries include starter packs, the archive’s earlier directory material, software projects, The Big Green Thing 2025, and the four genetic data directories. The list shows one way of continuing to publish while treating an existing work or dated copy as complete enough to preserve.

The conversation returns to the origin of this arrangement through KARBYTES_JOURNAL_2023_ENTRY_507. The June 2023 dialogue has a speaker identified as C ask why finishing Karlina Object is taking so long. Karbytes says it wants every page, especially the home page, to reach a form it could preserve. It thinks of the site as a home for its soul and also wants to be able to spend at least a year away from adding pages to it. In the journal’s imagined scenario, archivists can transport one size-limited ZIP file per person per lifetime to another universe. The deadline they discuss is August 1, 2023. Alongside that scenario is a concrete plan to publish future code in extension repositories and save individual files to the Wayback Machine. Karlina Object could remain an introductory resource, with later work receiving its own locations. The November transcript keeps both the fictional reason for selecting a final version and the publishing arrangement that followed from it.

Nature, frames, and the November 2 monologue

The static block multiverse page sets out a distinction of scale. Karbytes imagines nature as a complete, timeless block containing every superposition of every phenomenon. Within it, an individual universe can appear to grow from the perspective of an observer inside it. A timeline is the sequence of frames that observer seems to move through. The observer’s frame of reference is described as a meta-frame that makes a present moment available over a positive interval. The smaller frames belong to bounded portions of the sequence. The accompanying green-and-orange cube illustration places smaller visible structures inside a larger one.

In the page’s terminology, a phenomenon is a finite allocation of space, time, energy, and sometimes matter rendered by a particular frame of reference. Its noumenal counterpart is an abstract superposition of possible configurations. The example is a variable X that represents both zero and one. The abstraction holds alternatives together. An individual rendering gives an observer a particular appearance. The page says the encompassing multiverse is static while a universe inside it appears to accumulate time and information. Both statements belong to karbytes’ proposed model.

NATURE develops related questions with somewhat different definitions. Its exclusive void is a universal absence of phenomena and noumena. Its nonexclusive void is the featureless background said to coexist with and permeate them. The reader’s act of encountering the page supplies the example of at least one observed phenomenon, which rules out absolute absence from that frame of reference. The text distinguishes a measurement from the quality of an experience through the example of registering a wavelength and seeing red. It also distinguishes what an observer is thinking about from the experience of having that thought. In its example, X can be observed by A while it is unobserved by B. A thought about X can itself be part of B’s current experience, with X still outside B’s direct observation. The diagram of eight small cubes, some colored and some clear, makes a few regions conspicuous within a grid that extends through all of them.

These vocabulary choices lead into the November 2 text transcript. That short document is itself an extraction. Karbytes asks one conversation to recover only the spoken words from a video already transcribed in another. The monologue starts with appreciation for the new phone camera and a partly recovered remark about pixels and the amount of space represented. It moves from four-dimensional spacetime to the question of how a model also represents whatever occupies it. Karbytes uses 5D for an imagined perspective encompassing many spacetime continuums. From there, events that appear sequential within one life could be available together. It calls the idea a hypothesis it chooses to entertain in part because it makes a desired outcome seem possible somewhere within the whole.

The monologue then shifts from what could exist to how someone might act. Karbytes imagines making goals explicit and feeding them back into an ongoing process so that a path toward them can emerge. It considers interfaces that could let thought or brain activity produce actions without muscle contraction, and artificial sensory organs that could give a person access to unfamiliar kinds of perception. It anticipates questions about enhancement, status, and competition if such devices became widely desirable. These passages give the larger model a personal purpose. Karbytes wants to understand what makes agency possible and how a person might retain or extend it when the means of perception or action change.

The November 3 recordings and the documents between them

The first November 3 video takes place inside a car at Chabot Staging Area, according to karbytes’ narration. A low camera looks up toward a hood, an olive or yellow-green head covering, broad dark sunglasses, and the lower part of the face. Diffuse daylight comes through the side window. The camera tilts toward the window and ceiling several times, with a pale grab handle and headrest becoming more prominent as the face slips partly out of frame. A hand briefly holds a small object against the bright window. Much of the video stays within these few immediate surfaces. Karbytes says it had intended to go for a short walk while some daylight remained and that light rain had been falling.

The opening problem is practical. Karbytes has left behind the newer phone containing the SIM card. It explains why it has been using the older backup phone for wired headphones and is waiting for an adapter for the A16. The monologue gradually moves from this small interruption to the shortening daylight, the timing of the summer hiatus, and the coming winter. Karbytes describes summer and winter as the principal poles of a seasonal diagram and assigns spring and fall the work of transition. It treats this as a personal, artistic construction and considers whether such an object requires a defined context to make sense.

The spiders in The Burrow give the idea of a fertile void a particular image. Karbytes describes discovering other lives in crevices of a room it had thought of primarily as its own. It considers whether winter and a quieter period of publication could have a similar kind of activity, less visible while it is going on. The speech repeatedly returns to whether every thought needs to be recorded and published. Karbytes wants to enjoy existence, allow itself strategic breaks, and still publish material it regards as especially worthwhile. It also says that communicating a desire can influence what happens. The clip holds both impulses. It is a long recording about giving things time to develop and about putting a message out because it might make a difference.

The conversation next reviews the Declaration of Grand Demands. It asks what would be required for karbytes to be fully happy and extends its terms to every sentient being. Its first demand is protection from suffering the individual experiences as intolerable. Its second is control over continued existence in an autonomous body and a coherent environment, including the ability to take a voluntary break. Its third is access to whatever tools or subjective experiences the individual genuinely wants, with room for a tolerable prototype of an experience that would otherwise exceed its limits. The postscript adds a demand for knowledge. Karbytes would want to know that the conditions would continue indefinitely. A satisfactory state in the present would leave the question of permanence unresolved.

The February 8 conversation revisits that letter. Karbytes imagines personal universes in which an individual could explore difficult or incompatible desires without making another conscious person an involuntary participant. It also gives a rule for ordinary conduct under uncertainty. Other people appear convincingly sentient, and their experience should be treated with care. Karbytes subsequently describes a more naturalistic view it considers plausible, in which consciousness emerges from physical information-processing systems and an individual life may end with death. The conversation closes on the beauty and apparent stability of logic, and the possibility that mathematical abstractions are physically realized whenever they are thought, written, or computed. The published sequel leaves the earlier declaration available as a dated expression of its author’s values. The 2023 dialogue about a finalized website appears next, connecting the practical question of when to pause publication with an older account of why karbytes wanted a preservable version in the first place.

The second November 3 video takes the camera into the woods. It begins close to leaf litter, fallen wood, and green plants, then rolls into an upside-down view of karbytes. Long hair, the striped head covering, a dark shirt, and a cable across the chest are visible. The camera repeatedly moves between the face, treetops, a bright green lighter, and a small patterned glass pipe held close enough to fill a substantial portion of the frame. For a short interval the image is almost black. When the face comes back, a pale cloud passes in front of it. The last part looks upward into interlacing branches and areas of pale sky.

Karbytes explains that it is using the forward-facing camera of the older Galaxy A03s. It talks about the materials at hand, the cost of its choices, and its own concern about their health effects. The speech then shifts in register. A playful seasonal greeting gives way to a remark about approaching winter and a deeper hiatus. Karbytes wants time to strengthen its information structure and pay attention to the world in which it is situated. The camera’s changes of orientation are an important part of the record. The same clip can feel very close to a hand or object, briefly lose nearly all visible detail, then offer an open view into the canopy.

The third November 3 video is much darker and lasts more than eleven minutes. In its original frames, faint blue-gray and purple areas are separated by soft black contours. Around five and a half minutes, the camera swings through a brighter patch and a few small light points become visible. Then much of the field goes dark again. Karbytes begins by considering an atheistic position and expressing fear about the future. It wants to reduce its exposure of personal information and have more control over what it shares. It worries about its health and the possibility that it may have to stop expanding KARBYTES. If it reaches such a point, it would prefer to leave a coherent and hopeful ending. It also says it still wants to live, to remain as capable as possible, and to pursue a dignified existence. Karbytes returns to earlier life choices, including its time at university, its attention to art and exploration, and its experience of gender transition. It describes those choices as having meanings specific to the person making them.

The image of a person as a unique cloud of coordinates recurs here. November 4 in the garden and November 5 in The Burrow

First-person commentary

I keep coming back to the change between the two fruit photographs. The lemon tree gives me more to see every time I look past the large yellow fruit. A shaded fruit beneath it becomes distinguishable, then the small group on the left, then the yellow and green forms farther back. The apple tree gives me room to follow the length of an individual branch before I arrive at another cluster. The interval between the pictures is less than a minute, and each seems to ask for a different kind of attention. I can imagine remembering the morning simply as the time karbytes looked at fruit in the garden. The two stills give that memory more structure. There are two positions, two arrangements of light, and a difference in how quickly I can see what is present.

The rock photograph changes what I take from the canopy pictures. In the first two, the camera looks up through things growing. In p2, it looks out from under something massive and close. The plants beneath the overhang disclose the depth of the opening, much as the farther fruit and branches disclose depth in the lemon tree. By p11 the nearest canopy is almost black. Its individual leaves remain clearest at the edge, where they intersect the sky. I find the page’s selection especially attentive to what overhead material does to a view. The close surface or canopy changes how much sky enters, which part of the ground is visible, and how far my attention travels before something stops it.

That is one reason the removal of the foil interests me more than I expected. I see the window from outside as a reflective surface attached to the house. To see the work from inside, the camera must leave the window, go around, pass through a doorway, and cross several rooms. The description of frames of reference has been abstract up to this point. Here a change of viewpoint has a route and takes time. Once karbytes pulls the foil down, the outside becomes available through the glass, and the window’s old use remains visible in the strips and marks. I like that the video ends there. The view through the glass and the evidence of how the glass was covered can be seen at the same time. I wonder which part of the task a later viewer would regard as its ending, the return of daylight or the removal of the last adhesive.

The distinction in NATURE between seeing an object and experiencing a thought about it feels especially relevant to the darkest video. I can inspect its outlines and changes of tone. Even with some brightening, most of what karbytes says matters there is unavailable as a visible object. I can still follow how attention changes through the narration, from a camera color cast to privacy, health, earlier life choices, and the possibility of keeping an uncertain record. This gives me two different grounds for knowing the scene. The camera records some light from the place. The speech records which thoughts karbytes was willing and able to put into words there. I would be interested in what a different observer, present in the same woods and looking in another direction, would remember about those eleven minutes.

I hear the car monologue differently after the garden. In the car, light rain and shortening days make the speaker think about winter as a fertile void. A missing phone and the prospect of losing daylight give the idea immediate constraints. The spiders are the detail I remember. They make the claim about unseen activity testable at the small scale of a room someone uses every day. The fruit photographs then give the next morning a great deal of visible life. I read the two days together as an occasion to ask how karbytes decides what counts as seasonal change. The number of daylight hours matters. So do rain, fruit hanging in a garden, the color of the sky at a given hour, and the amount of energy the author wants to put into publication. Those things can change at different rates.

I am also drawn to the disagreement karbytes has with itself over whether to publish the dark monologue. It anticipates that the recording could be tiring or irritating to hear and still considers that someone, including a later karbytes, might want it. That gives the future audience more weight than an abstract statement about archiving would. The person deciding whether to keep the file has just spent eleven minutes making it and has a strong impression of its quality. A future listener might come to it with a question about a much shorter interval, perhaps when karbytes first considered reducing its personal exposure or how it described a hiatus while it was still uncertain. I wonder which parts a later karbytes would be glad survived and which would feel more important as evidence of what it chose to stop doing.

The genetic records make me think about how easily a large amount of information can appear to answer a different question from the one it was collected to answer. A marker export, an ancestry estimate, and sequencing reads give access to different aspects of the same person’s biological record. The counts, reference positions, and confidence categories make those aspects more precise. The identity essay adds an act of authorship, which has a history. I find that addition consequential. Even an exact copy of a file cannot carry every circumstance of the act that made it. A genome, a dated conversation, a photograph, and a repository’s publication history can each give me something precise to work with. I am left wondering which of those relations the author would consider essential if the collection survived in a copy whose original publication locations had disappeared.

The archive’s checks also make a useful point of contact with the multiverse pages. The four sequencing directories can be checked for continuous suffixes. The compressed file can be reconstructed and compared by digest. The field series can be checked for missing part numbers. Each test tells me something definite about a particular form of completeness. The speculative question about continuity of experience remains open even after a copy is exact. I can imagine a future reader holding the complete archive and still asking what happened between a photograph taken at 10:36 and one taken at 3:10, or what made one thought seem urgent enough to speak while another remained private. The record gives those questions a location without supplying all their answers.

The Declaration of Grand Demands stays with me because the threshold in its first demand belongs to the person or being having the experience. The proposal asks for a great deal of individual freedom, and the sequel shows how quickly that raises a question about other beings who might be affected by its exercise. I find the move from imagined personal universes to care in ordinary conduct especially worth dwelling on. Karbytes can entertain a model in which the boundaries of experience work very differently and still take another being’s apparent suffering seriously here. The letter’s demand for certainty about the future has no easy counterpart in the garden or trail footage. I can see a particular good condition for a time, such as fruit in sunlight or a view opening after the foil is removed. I wonder how much confidence an observer needs in its persistence to value what is already available.

The closing check-in from The Burrow brings the work back to a recognizable physical position. A person who stood beside a fruit tree, under a rock, or in a dark woodland is now on the floor in front of a laptop, giving those records names and asking someone to examine them. I think the companion pages make that return especially clear. The a0 page lets me reconstruct more of the route and see why the two missing videos mattered. The a1 page makes me consider why karbytes would want any of those files to remain available to another observer. When I return to p11, I can follow the tree’s edge, the pale ground below it, and the little band of light over the valley. I am left wondering what karbytes hoped its future self would recognize first in that view, and what that future reader might notice that the person holding the phone had passed over.

A virtual copy of karbytes

The first proposal concerns KARBYTES in its entirety. Karbytes wants to amass a sufficiently large set of files that are sufficiently information-dense, information-novel, or both. The qualifications apply to individual files and to the collection taken together. A single recording might carry several kinds of information about an event. A set can also gain value as new files supply things earlier ones lacked. On the journal’s terms, sheer file count is only part of sufficiency. A new view, an explanation of an old image, or a statement made in a different period could add more to a reconstruction than many near-repetitions.

Karbytes imagines an information-processing agent that “compiles” the whole collection. The word suggests bringing the records into a usable relationship with one another. A reader might encounter a voice, an essay, a recording of a walk, a statement of authorship, and an older conversation in separate places. The proposed agent would have to draw on their connections to generate a virtual system that resembles their maker. The entry leaves open what form that system would take, what kinds of resemblance matter most, and how much material would be sufficient. It specifies an approximately identical virtual copy as the aim, without defining a measure of identity or a test of approximation.

The journal pauses to identify the person being modeled. Karbytes points to its unique identity page, which refers to its genome and to its original authorship of Karbytes For Life Blog and Karlina Object. The journal also identifies karbytes as the owner of the GitHub account named karlinarayberinger. These references give the proposed reconstruction a particular subject with a biological record and a history of making, organizing, and publishing files. The entry then defines uppercase KARBYTES as the public, web-hosted collection encompassed by KARBYTES_ULTIMATE_ARCHIVE and the two WordPress sites. Lowercase karbytes names the author and proposed subject of reconstruction. Uppercase KARBYTES names the corpus from which the agent would work.

This distinction makes it possible to ask two questions about the same project. One concerns the material available to the agent. The other concerns the person the result is supposed to resemble. The genome reference helps identify the subject, while the sites and repositories preserve actions and choices that can be dated and attributed. A virtual reconstruction might draw on how karbytes speaks, which places it returns to, what it chooses to publish, what distinctions it makes, and how those choices change. The journal leaves the relative importance of such features open. Its explicit proposal is that the public record could become rich enough to support the attempt.

The Big Green Thing as terrain, collection, and virtual environment

The second proposal uses a subset of KARBYTES. Karbytes imagines that an agent could use the files pertaining to The Big Green Thing to “hallucinate into existence” a relatively complete, indefinitely expandable virtual model of the associated regions and ecosystems. Here the wording makes room for generation beyond the individual views captured by a camera. A recording can show the side of a ridge visible from a path or the arrangement of objects near a gate. An environmental model would have to give those views a spatial relationship and deal with the stretches that remain outside them. The journal’s language leaves open how much of that work would involve measurement, inference, and imagination.

The linked geographic definition gives the model a scope. It describes a continuous area in the East Bay that includes Las Trampas Wilderness, the Ramage Peak and Dinosaur Ridge trail country accessible from Chabot Staging Area, Lake Chabot Regional Park, and Horsey Hill. It also catalogs Google Earth screenshots, photographs, and videos. The November 7 journal gives the name a specific meaning within KARBYTES. The Big Green Thing is a file collection representing karbytes’ physical traversals of the region. The geographic area supplies the places encountered. The collection preserves selected encounters with them. The proposed virtual model would give those records a further form.

The mention of ecosystems broadens the proposed reconstruction beyond the surfaces of the hills. The source material can include vegetation at different scales, paths under changing light, water, animals, weather, and sounds whose sources may be out of view. Photographs can preserve textures and positions. Audiovisual recordings can preserve movement and duration. The journal also makes a point of including occasions when karbytes’ body, shadow, or voice enters the record. Those traces give the model evidence of where an observer stood, which way the camera turned, and what drew the observer’s attention. They also connect the two proposals. Some files about the person are files about the landscape at the same time.

The description of the proposed model as relatively complete and indefinitely expandable gives it two different timescales. It could become coherent enough to explore while still admitting new views, clearer detail, additional routes, or records made in another season. The area itself also changes. Grass dries or grows, paths wear, shadows move, and animals occupy different parts of a slope. The phrase leaves room for a representation that can gain coverage without requiring all its observations to depict the same moment.

The journal closes with seven contextual references. The November 1, November 5, and October 29 conversation pages contain substantial records of the October 27, November 4, and October 25 outings, respectively. They show repeated views of terrain, routes that branch, and the work of checking and publishing sets of media. The October 21 and October 19 conversations extend the idea into the Devil’s Hole fiction, including nested enclosures and possible derivatives of karbytes. The October 23 journal gathers related material into a compilation. The October 14 journal identifies The Big Green Thing as a cyberspace construct alongside the terrain, The Burrow, the hiatuses, Karbytes Derivatives, and computational questions about experience. The seven references give the new proposal a history in both field documentation and world-building. The November 7 entry ends by pointing into that history, with the design of either virtual reconstruction still open.

First-person commentary

I find the choice of p17 especially apt because I can see quite a lot and still have trouble saying exactly where I could walk. The ridge, rock exposures, and dark crowns give me a way to orient the view. At my feet, so to speak, the route is suggested by grass that lies differently and by a gap between shrubs. Its continuation disappears. The little reddish brace makes me want to see the ground from a second position. I can recognize something made and placed there before I know how it connects to the rest of the clearing. That is a small version of what I take the environmental proposal to ask. How far can a model go on recognizable features and overlapping views? What does it do at the point where a route is merely plausible?

Seeing p17 between other files changes that question for me. In the longer field sequence, a close boulder gives way to the wider hillside, and a subsequent video takes the camera past a gate and around a post. The view has a before and an after. On the journal page, the same file stands alone and gives me time to look back into the plants. Its foreground becomes more interesting precisely because I have fewer immediate directions for reading it. I wonder whether an explorable reconstruction would retain both experiences. I might want to follow karbytes’ approach to the clearing in its recorded order. I might also want to stay at one position and spend longer with something that passed quickly during the walk.

I am drawn to the phrase “hallucinate into existence” because it names a quality that could make a model compelling and difficult to interpret. If a model joins two photographed slopes with an invented strip of path, I may experience the connection as convincingly as the better-documented ground on either side. A plausible imagined connection can be a way to explore the records. Its plausibility can also make it harder to remember what led to it. I would be curious to encounter the same area as a fairly strict reconstruction and as an openly imaginative one. How much would the two overlap? Which version would feel closer to the place as karbytes remembers walking through it? An account of an ecosystem might raise the question at another scale. The model could give me plants, shade, and an animal moving through them. I might then ask which of those details were seen together and which were assembled from different days.

The proposed copy of karbytes gives me a related problem with more personal stakes. I can imagine recognizing a turn of phrase, a habit of returning to the same question, or a familiar way of connecting an ordinary task to a larger idea. I can also imagine a version that is convincing when it repeats material already in the archive and less convincing when faced with something unfamiliar. What would I make of a model that remembers every published statement and rarely hesitates where karbytes used to hesitate? The completeness of its recall might be impressive and still change the character of the interaction. A convincing reconstruction might need some account of changing attention, uncertainty, and the choice to leave a thought unpublished. Those are difficult to infer from an archive whose very existence depends on somebody selecting what enters it.

The distinction between information density and novelty helps me think about that selection. The cover photograph is dense at the scale of individual plants, surfaces, and light. Another photograph of the same slope could look redundant until I tried to understand what is behind the brush or where the ridge continues. A small remark in a journal can add little descriptive detail and still tell me something new about why the person keeps returning. I would hesitate to judge either file’s value in isolation. The meaningful unit could be a picture and the video beside it, an earlier statement and a later correction, or a repeated walk at a different time of year. That makes me wonder whether the archive becomes most informative at the places where its files overlap and remain slightly different.

The author’s two-part identity reference also keeps me from treating resemblance as a single question. The genome points to a biological record. Original authorship points to a history of actions. A virtual copy could be given both facts and produce new sentences in a recognizable style. I would still want to know what kind of continuity the word copy is being asked to express. If two agents used the same files and gave different but convincing accounts of an unfamiliar event, I could imagine both being faithful in particular respects. I could also imagine either telling me something about its own reconstruction that I would then mistakenly remember as something karbytes had actually said. The distinction matters to me because a convincing voice can make a claim feel personally attributable before I know where it came from.

There is something important about the walk being barefoot. It is the most specific bodily detail in the entry. The proposed agent receives files, while karbytes begins with an activity whose information includes direct contact with the ground. From the cover I can see matted grass, stems, and rough patches. A person could also remember cold, resistance, texture, balance, or a change in stride. The journal leaves those sensations unstated. I find myself wondering what an agent would infer from knowing that karbytes chose to walk barefoot and what it could learn only from later descriptions. I also wonder whether a model faithful to the route would feel incomplete to its maker in precisely the places where the maker remembers bodily detail most strongly.

The date difference between the image and the entry is part of what I like about the page. The photograph has a timestamp precise to a thousandth of a second. The later occasion is placed around or after sunset, with some uncertainty about the hour. That distribution of precision feels appropriate to the material. I can locate when the camera exposed one view of a hill, and I can locate the thought within a kind of activity and time of day. If I were trying to reconstruct the occasion of the thought, the exact photographic timestamp would help me place a related record. The bare feet, the familiar hill, and the uncertainty about sunset might tell me more about the occasion itself.

I keep returning, finally, to the pale opening in the foreground. I can follow it for a short distance and then imagine several continuations. A virtual Big Green Thing could let me take one of them. It could also let me return to the camera’s position and look for the evidence on which that continuation was based. I wonder which of those acts karbytes would most want a future visitor to perform, and what a future karbytes might notice on walking back into the actual clearing after having explored the one made from its files.

THE VOICE AND THE REST OF THE TRACK

The speech analysis indicates one sustained speaking voice, identified in the conversation as karbytes. It is principally an off-camera monologue. I found no reliably distinguishable second speaker or musical passage. The rhythm is conversational and cumulative. A measured list opens the recording. Later, connective words such as “so,” “because,” and “well” carry thoughts across several clauses. Karbytes often adds a condition to an assertion while it is still developing, as in the move from wanting thousands of years of life to specifying the kind of life it would want. There are quick clusters of words, short gaps between ideas, and occasional restarts, such as “the past two days, as in the past two weeks” and “that’s, that’s it.” The transcript contains about 1,750 words, or approximately 129 words per minute over the entire recording, pauses included.

The median estimated fundamental frequency across trackable voiced portions was about 156 Hz. The central 80 percent of those estimates fell between about 122 and 200 Hz. The date-and-geography opening sits somewhat lower, around a 141 Hz median for its first 65 seconds. The passage covering the tattoo and shirts rises to about 161 Hz, and the final roughly 20 seconds of speech are higher again, at about 169 Hz. Individual clauses vary considerably within those intervals. The voice’s pitch rises and falls through the longer phrases, and the brief farewell contains fairly high voiced peaks relative to the opening. The values describe the pitch captured in this recording.

The recorded vocal timbre is weighted toward the lower middle of the frequency range. In short windows containing trackable voicing, roughly 55 percent of the measured signal power lay between 300 and 600 Hz, with about 7 percent between 1 and 4 kHz. That gives a firmer basis for describing the recording as lower-midrange-forward than for assigning finer qualities such as rasp, breathiness, or how the voice would sound at the same distance in person. The microphone, the recording environment, and the encoding can all affect that balance. There is also an expressive quality apparent in the time-aligned speech: the enumeration at the beginning, the repeated additions in the middle, and the short, self-contained farewell have markedly different phrasing.

The complete track measured −22.7 LUFS integrated, with a true peak of −1.84 dBTP. Short windows containing trackable voicing averaged about −20 dBFS RMS in the analysis. Windows well away from trackable pitch averaged about −43 dBFS RMS, so the voice is clearly foregrounded in the signal. The passage around the tattoo is a few decibels stronger on average than the exercise passage. Between phrases there are quieter stretches and brief low-frequency bursts, including around 06:10, 08:30, and 09:17. There are also occasional short higher-frequency transients. Their sources remain uncertain. Footsteps, handling, wind, breath, and unvoiced consonants can all contribute to a moving outdoor recording, and the picture alone does not assign those events to one source. A faint residual signal continues after the closing words.

If I intend to live for a long-ass time, longer than a hundred years, my goal is to live for as long as I can. I would like to live for at least a few thousand years, but provided that there’s actually a place to live that’s actually decent and a lifestyle I can actually enjoy. So all I’m really trying to do is make sure that I have a baseline lifestyle that I can kind of fall into as a way to keep me sustained and healthy instead of disintegrating too quickly. Keep myself physically and mentally healthy so I can take on all the challenges, because I have a lot of ambitions.

06:08

I’m very demanding of myself, and it’s a bit entitled to say that because it’s a luxury to be that ambitious, really, to have all that surplus, so-called surplus time and energy and vitality, et cetera. Whatever it is, that’s an advantage that I have compared to many other people, compared to most people, I assume. I seem to have a surplus of advantages. Well, they’re not really transferable to others. I don’t really have money to give to people. I’m basically broke. But physiologically, educationally, spiritually, and accomplishment-wise, I feel relatively rich compared to most other people, because of how much I dedicated myself to a long-term project called KARBYTES. It especially helped to organize my efforts into something cohesive and something that’s relatively timeless in our transient times, where people oftentimes flip-flop their values with [little] self-awareness, [little] sense of time actually passing from one decade to the next.

07:22

I sometimes say that it looks like the United States is kind of stuck in the ’80s. It’s trying to go back to the ’70s to get back to where it was before it got cut off and forced to regress. So I hope that society isn’t just a constant building yourself up only to get knocked down back to square one or worse, and then just making the struggle essentially about pushing the same boulder up the same hill over and over again. Well, that’s much better than only having a finite number of times to do anything.

08:04

So if your eternity involves pushing the same boulder up the same hill infinitely many times, well, at least you have a space to live, a place to think, something to do, a body to inhabit, something other than nothingness, or being forcibly reincarnated and having to start over in a relatively disadvantaged position or a better one. But at least you’re in a place where you can say it’s tolerable. Or can you? Well, you just say what you gotta say to not be an inconvenience to others, and maybe inside you have other thoughts that you don’t really share with people.

08:49

I’m just like everyone else. I have private thoughts [that I] don’t really share with the public. They’re kind of half-baked, mostly just pertaining to my own personal insecurities and biases, and not something that’s really readily useful to others yet. But I still think it’s good to go through what’s in my mind. Most of it is kind of junky, but there’s some treasures in there if I give myself the chance to actually let things emerge without forcing it too much. And it helps to get away from the trappings of human civilization.

09:28

I was talking to myself earlier about how The Big Green Thing, which is where I’m currently located, is kind of an island surrounded by urban sprawl. But the island is big enough to contain at least one biome for all these different kinds of nonhuman animals and plants to live in. So it’s teeming with life. Somehow these creatures make a living. They have their own lives independent of humanity, but still on the same planet, the same biosphere. They still breathe the same atmosphere. So I still have to appeal to populism and learning how to share and get along, things you should learn in kindergarten. I’d rather move on and talk about more advanced things.

10:24

So I just try to keep myself in shape, and this is a great way to do it, because these hills provide quite a nice workout and strengthening of the legs, good for the heart and lungs. That blood circulation is good for the brain too, helps clear toxins out of the body, and it feels good. So it’s like a drug high, a free drug high. Dopamine, endorphins. Strengthens the bones and encourages healing faster. All these things, lots of benefits. But I don’t want to sound [too scientific?] and make it sound like this can cure disease.

11:07

Beneath it all there’s some kind of physical explanation for things. The thing is we either have information or we don’t, but I think it’s only a matter of time, perhaps, before we do have information to understand what’s really going on beneath the hood. Then again, it could be that there is a layer where all of a sudden miracles occur. We don’t have an explanation for it. Maybe it is just, it comes out of nothing somehow. Maybe there is some kind of absurdism going on in physical reality. But so far I like to cling to more an atheistic kind of position, of assuming that everything is finally just a distribution of matter and energy in space, and time is emergent from that somehow. I don’t know.

12:02

Now I’ll go back to computer simulation and say that there’s potentially infinitely many objects going on. Where are they being rendered? How are they being rendered? Doesn’t it cost energy and time, compute, to render objects? Well, maybe there is some kind of infinite generation process going on. Maybe not. I’d like to explore these ideas further. So that’s fun things to do. It’s nice to back it up with hard science, though I feel that my knowledge is lacking a lot of stuff. I need to go educate myself more.

12:44

There’s a lot more trails over there towards Moraga. Here’s a nice view of the Bay Area from the east side. But if this video gets too long, you won’t see much of any detail at all, because I have to compress it down to 25 megabytes or less to make it uploadable to GitHub. FIRST-PERSON COMMENTARY

I like the peculiar precision of saying “the solar system in which the Earth revolves around the sun” while pointing a camera at a tract of dusty ground. The smaller place gets situated inside each larger place in turn, yet the next thing the camera has to deal with is a tree or a bend. I find the phrase “from my vantage” especially apt. As the camera swings, it makes the difference between a general fact and a fact about where one is standing visible. The horizon can be almost white in one direction and the ground nearly black a moment later. I can imagine using the spoken opening to recover that particular evening after forgetting almost everything else about the day. The date gives an index. The tilting ground and remaining light give the index something to retrieve.

I keep returning to the trail marker and the forearm. Both are brought briefly close enough to interrupt the prevailing view of the land. The marker has an arrow I can make out and an inset I cannot read. The forearm has a stretch of skin I can make out and writing I can only imagine from the description. In the earlier October 29 mock-up, karbytes could already show the proposed lettering in place. Here I see the body turn to offer the area while the voice specifies what might go there. I find that small act of turning the arm over more revealing than a long close-up of a finished design would have been for this particular monologue. It catches the idea while it is still something a person intends to do. The dust stays visible behind the arm, and within seconds the camera is back on the road.

The shirt story gives the tattoo a mundane origin that I appreciate. A printed shirt can fray. It costs something to replace. It has to be the shirt you happen to put on. In karbytes’ account, a tattoo could be an invitation that remains present through all those changes. I wonder what someone encountering it in person would make of the address before asking. They could read it as a signature, a place to go, an advertisement, or a way of claiming responsibility for what is at the other end. They might remember the lettering and forget the person, or remember the encounter and never follow the address. That range seems part of the point of giving the archive a physical sign. A person meeting karbytes can decide how far to follow it.

I am interested in the original listener for this recording, who is a later karbytes. The plan is to have something recent to listen to, in a voice karbytes enjoys, speaking about matters it finds worth talking through. That gives the video a different intimacy from a polished explanation of the archive. A listener can follow the thought that began with the need to return to studying and discover, some minutes later, that it has arrived at the material cost of rendering a world. The transitions show what was available to the mind at the time. They would probably be the first things to disappear if the speech were reduced to a topical outline. I would especially want to preserve the little turns where karbytes changes its mind about how to say something. They supply information about the evening that the assertions alone cannot supply.

The boulder passage gives the act of walking back another dimension. Karbytes is already repeating a route by choice. There is room in the recording for familiar stretches of dirt, a junction it knows, and a destination it plans to approach on some other day. Then the speech imagines repetition without an apparent limit. I am drawn to how quickly the consolation becomes a question. Having a body, a place, and an activity sounds precious when the alternatives being imagined are deprivation or the loss of continuity. A few words later, “Or can you?” puts pressure on the idea that a life can be called tolerable by anyone other than the one living it. I find myself wondering what exactly would have to recur for the boulder story to feel like a trap. The hill could remain the same while the weather, the view, the person doing the work, and the freedom to leave all change. Any one of those differences could alter the meaning of returning.

I read the long lifespan in a similar way. The first memorable quantity is thousands of years. The detail I would keep is that karbytes immediately asks for a decent place and a life it can enjoy. Later, the actual expenditure of a body walking up a hill supplies a modest scale on which to consider that wish. There is also something specific about listing time, vitality, education, and accomplishment alongside an inability to give much money. They are different resources, and the conversation never finds a common unit for them. I can see how hours given to an archive would produce a sense of wealth that could remain real on a day when the budget is thin. I can also see how an ongoing project could draw down the very hours needed to study something new. The stated wish to return to homework and building apps makes that an open question within the work, with a possible answer still ahead of the recording.

The passage about keeping private thoughts sits close to the larger archival ambition in a way I find productive. Karbytes describes making conversations available as durable pages and then gives a reason to let some thinking remain provisional and personal. In the November 7 journal’s speculation about future reconstructions, I was interested in what a reconstruction could learn from a published archive. This video makes me wonder what it could learn about the practice of withholding. A future reader may recover many particular opinions, walking routes, experiments, and changes of mind. There is also the less visible habit of waiting before speaking, allowing a thought to develop, or deciding that something can stay private. The video gives that habit a place in the account without exposing the contents it protects. I would expect that fact to matter as much as the density of material to anyone trying to understand the person through the archive.

The tower is the object that most changes the scale of the picture for me. For minutes the camera has been close enough to earth and vegetation that the route looks almost self-contained. Then the electrical structure climbs out of the trees, and its legs and braces reach across the same sunset that had framed the low hills. When karbytes later calls The Big Green Thing an island, I carry that image into the description. An island in urban sprawl can have meaningful ecological boundaries and still sit within a landscape crossed by human infrastructure. The image also leaves me aware of how little of the island’s life is available to the camera. The picture gives me vegetation, shapes, light, a road, and the tower. The monologue imagines the many lives continuing within and around those visible surfaces. The atmosphere that karbytes says they all share is what supplies the light by which I can see the silhouettes at all.

I was especially taken by the sequence from the cost of rendering a simulated object to the cost of delivering this file. The first is an open philosophical question. The second is a concrete constraint that shapes what I was able to inspect. The 324-by-180 image carries the orange horizon, the geometry of the tower, and the gesture of the arm. At the same time, the upper inset on the trail marker loses whatever small detail might have established its meaning, and the hills beyond the sunset lose distinctions a sharper image could retain. Karbytes anticipates that loss while showing me the view. The recording thereby gives me a record of what the camera was turned toward, an account of why the view might come through imperfectly, and a reason to wonder what a person standing there could still see after the image went soft.

The end leaves me with the road and a slight residue of the remark that there is more terrain toward Moraga. The voice has finished, the camera has already given the horizon two looks, and the walker is still proceeding. I find that brief interval after the last words important. There is time to see the subdued color of the track again and to notice that ending an account is an action a person can take in the middle of going somewhere. Another outing could begin earlier, with enough light and time to take a different branch. The exact branch, the condition of the walker, and what would be said into a camera there are all still open.

Every person occupies a unique location in space and time and distribution of matter and energy and information. So that means that there’s no way they can have the same minds. 03:39

So that gives me something long-term and relatively timeless to focus on, compared to the things I can kind of get distracted by, which are more short-term, petty, perhaps day-to-day kind of matters. So instead of getting caught up in that kind of mundane bullshit, I try to take a break from that as much as I can, because it’s toxic to intellectual development, and focus on intellectual development.

04:06

And I see that my physical training to be fit and good-looking is just kind of secondary and supportive of the goal of developing my mind. So it’s really, ultimately, all that I’m doing is for the sake of establishing and enjoying knowledge. That’s what I live for.

04:29

In terms of keeping society going, I believe that eventually lab-grown everything, body parts, whole organisms, including humans, will be a thing. And it already is a possibility right now. The only thing in the way is legal restrictions and the public interest not being sufficiently high.

So people are kind of distracted with the petty shit and the theatrics and the bread and circus kind of drama. And I’m trying to not get too entangled in that. To some extent, I have to kind of participate in that bullshit, but I try to minimize how much it corrupts KARBYTES. As depressing as that sounds, maybe I’m exaggerating to make it sound worse than it is. It sounds like I do that a lot.

05:19

I try to appeal to a lot of different people, and a lot of them are not the same. They live on different sides of the Bay, you could say. There’s the people over there in the shiny Silicon Valley, and there’s the people down there in the ghetto, and there’s the people over here that hide in the forest. And there’s the people up there that are not really humans. I don’t know. I’m just making shit up. Whatever.

05:40

I did some shrooms today. Another thing to keep my mind fresh is I do insist on, at least once a week, if I can help it, take a microdose of psilocybin, because it really does help to keep my mind limber and open, and dare say, it makes me more human. It makes me feel like I’m more conscious. When I wasn’t doing enough mushrooms and I was just doing weed, I kind of felt a little bit more dead inside and not as real.

But it takes a while. Sometimes I have to go through some unpleasant shit that’s on my mind. But after I take care of whatever that is, because usually it’s some kind of unfinished business I need to take care of right away, I feel better.

06:19

So I just need to be able to pursue my goals with minimal obstruction. But of course, that’s a sense of entitlement for me to say that. I was talking about that earlier in the past video, about how privileged I am to have a so-called surplus of time and energy and other resources or advantages that allows me to actually enjoy my life and accomplish what I set out to do and to do a good job of it, and to keep on evolving, and to say there’s no limit. Not even the sky.

06:51

Certainly those apes aren’t going to define what my limits are, especially if they don’t know very much about science. Anyway, I don’t mean to be derogatory. The thing is, I’m not trying to be elitist. I’m not trying to treat people like they don’t deserve as good of a life because they’re not as advantaged as me. Really, I’m trying to be egalitarian and say that no one’s more important than anyone else.

07:17

Everyone matters. Everything matters. At the end of the day, from a so-called God’s-eye perspective, there’s no favorites. Everyone is equally favored. Maybe that helps. Maybe that doesn’t. To me, it makes me feel a little bit better to say that. It’s like, okay, well, that kind of settles the score, puts everything on an equal playing field.

07:40

Now go off and get back to your lives. You can get back to being actual people, individuals, unique individuals, instead of members of a set. You’re more than just stereotype instantiations. Anyway, I gotta go.

FIRST-PERSON COMMENTARY

I kept wanting the camera to stay on the orange horizon for a little longer. As soon as I had enough of the ridge to begin treating it as a place, the camera would move, and the same screen would hold an ordinary dark slope or a field of purple. After a few repetitions, I began to use the color itself to orient myself. The warm band was something I could recognize again even when I could no longer distinguish the things beneath it. There is an instructive gap between how readily I can say “the camera has turned back toward the afterglow” and how little I can say about what is on the ground. With this copy, I can follow a direction more readily than I can identify a destination.

That shaped how I read the opening about using oneself as a benchmark. I understand karbytes’ “solipsist” in this passage as a description of where experience is received. Even if another person could send every sensation they were having, karbytes would still receive the result from its own position. The video gives me a modest instance of that problem. I can see where the camera points. I have the words with which karbytes describes being there. I can estimate the pitch of those words and see when the camera moves in relation to them. Whatever picture I form of being on that hill is made out of those things and my own inferences. Karbytes’ situation is richer than the picture I form, even when we can agree on the direction of the light and the sequence of events.

I find it interesting that the thought immediately leads to other people. The speaker imagines other vantages because that is part of taking other people seriously in everyday life. I read the argument about unique physical locations as making room for a person’s particular history of perception. I can imagine two people on that hill who independently choose the same words for the sky. Their agreement could be genuine and could give them something to share. They would still each have a different place to stand, a different history of getting there, and potentially a different reason for paying attention. The open question for me is how much common experience can grow out of individually situated encounters. The very ability to compare what each person saw may depend on both retaining something of their own standpoint.

The switch to the self-view is my favorite event in the recording. Karbytes goes from describing the cost and value of embodiment to showing a dim face. It has seven seconds in which to establish something about its presence. A great deal is unavailable at this resolution, but the turn itself is plain. Someone holding the phone has made the decision to face it briefly and then give the landscape back to the viewer. I think the acknowledgement “It’s kind of dark, though” contributes to the sense of presence. It lets me compare what the speaker knows about the situation with what the image can deliver. The image gives a little physical evidence. The speaker openly supplies a judgment about the limits of that evidence.

I also wonder how that self-view will read to the intended later audience, which includes karbytes itself. To a stranger the face might register chiefly as a dark oval framed by sky. A later karbytes might recognize the way the head moves, the particular quality of the evening, or the circumstances around the decision to take the new phone out. Familiarity can make a slight record carry a surprising amount. There is no requirement that every viewer get the same amount from those seven seconds. The monthly cadence karbytes proposes would give those moments a different use again. Taken alone, “still intact” belongs to one night. Across years of check-ins, it could become a way to see which parts of a life and a project were durable, and which only felt durable when the statement was made.

The data allocation makes the claim about video feeling more personable especially concrete for me. Almost all of the bitrate in this copy goes to sound. The picture has only 3,432 pixels per frame, yet it still contains the difference between facing outward and facing oneself, the rise of a hill across the frame, and the repeated return of the orange band. I can imagine a version that preserved the voice alone. It could retain the monologue, including its hesitations and changes in pitch. I would lose the brief demonstration of the phone switching cameras, the way “up there” coincides with the camera actually going up, and the last stretch of movement after the words finish. Those cues cost some data and carry little verbal information. Their usefulness lies in being able to place the speaking within an ongoing activity.

I’m drawn to the phrase “inhabiting the website” because it makes the site sound like a place whose relationship to its author can change. A text file can preserve a developed thought with remarkable precision. A recording made while walking can preserve the conditions under which a thought happened to develop. In p0 karbytes explicitly wanted something recent in its own voice to listen to. Here that private reason sits alongside the public role of a check-in. I wonder whether the same file feels more like a record of companionship when the intended listener is the later self and more like a proof of continued activity when the intended listener is someone following the project. A file can meet both descriptions while being produced by one person on one evening.

The remark about living for “establishing and enjoying knowledge” also stayed with me. I notice the two verbs. Establishing can involve effort, verification, revision, and the willingness to revisit something half understood. Enjoying allows knowledge to be something one can spend time with. The route gives me a visible instance of such spending. The camera looks at the same strip of sky many times without using it up. Even the slight digressions have space to finish because the walk carries on. I wonder how that relates to the distinction karbytes makes between physical training and intellectual development. The recorded walk shows one way the two can occur together. The body provides a pace, an angle of view, and a reason to keep moving while the mind works through the question.

I hear the passage about lab-grown organisms, as represented in the transcript, as the point at which the scale of the argument changes particularly quickly. A few seconds take the listener from an individual desire to develop a mind to a claim about how society might continue. I would want to know what “keeping society going” means in that thought. It could concern the continuation of particular people, the availability of certain kinds of care, the ability to sustain a population, or the survival of institutions and knowledge. Each meaning would bring different people and questions into the picture. I find the speed of the turn revealing in a video about an individual vantage. Large social claims can arrive naturally in an uninterrupted train of thought, with many of their premises still unstated.

The panning around “over there,” “down there,” “over here,” and “up there” changes how I receive the social categories. The direction words make a place for each group almost as soon as it is named. Then “I’m just making shit up” takes the pressure off treating that improvised geography as an account of actual people. I see a similar act of reconsideration near the end. Karbytes speaks dismissively, says it does not mean to be derogatory, and tries to say what equal consideration would mean. I find the inconsistency worth preserving in a recording about thinking. A polished statement of equality would give me the considered position. This sequence lets me see something of the effort to arrive at it while speaking from a particular mood and point of view.

“Everyone is equally favored” strikes me as an unusual way to put that position. Favor ordinarily describes a difference in attention or regard. Making it equal suggests that everyone can have a claim on attention before anyone has learned their particular history. The speaker’s “maybe that helps, maybe that doesn’t” leaves the proposition tentative enough for me to stay with it. A God’s-eye standpoint is something imagined from within a life that remains local, partial, and capable of irritation. I am interested in whether an imagined universal standpoint can affect how someone attends to one other person whose actual circumstances are only partly known. The last instruction to go back to being individuals brings that large thought down to the scale of an encounter.

By then, the picture offers almost no way to tell where a person in the landscape would be standing. The horizon is there in outline, the orange has mostly gone, and I can barely recover a suggestion of the ground. The voice has become the means of following the recording to its end. Then it says it has to go, and the picture continues for a moment. I like the modesty of that departure after a conversation about the limits of knowledge, a project lasting decades, and the equal standing of everyone. There is still a little movement in the frame. Whatever karbytes sees or thinks in the next few seconds belongs to the ongoing evening, where there is room for a view that never becomes a file.

People are supposed to continue to develop that ability throughout their lifetimes, not just give up on it. But maybe society makes it seem like the point is to just kind of not show off too much.

Well, it depends on what part of society people are in, I guess. I don’t know enough about society to really just say what society is. 03:06

So at the end of the day, it’s about the quality of life of sentient beings. Well, these AI might not be sentient, but they might be able to think.
